On categories of monads and comonads in double categories
Vasileios Aravantinos-Sotiropoulos, Theofilos Tsantilas, Christina Vasilakopoulou

Abstract
. As is well known in the literature, the category 𝖬𝗈𝗇 ⁡ ( 𝒱 ) \mathsf{Mon}(\mathcal{V}) of monoids in a monoidal category 𝒱 \mathcal{V} satisfies various fundamental categorical properties, at least when the monoidal base 𝒱 \mathcal{V} is correspondingly well-behaved. In particular, 𝖬𝗈𝗇 ⁡ ( 𝒱 ) \mathsf{Mon}(\mathcal{V}) is monadic over 𝒱 \mathcal{V} as soon as free monoids exist, while if 𝒱 \mathcal{V} is cocomplete or locally presentable and its tensor ⊗ \otimes is sufficiently compatible with the appropriate colimits, then 𝖬𝗈𝗇 ⁡ ( 𝒱 ) \mathsf{Mon}(\mathcal{V}) inherits the analogous property. In the present work, we extend such results to the context of double categories. More precisely, we identify conditions on a double category 𝔻 \mathbb{D} under which one can show that the category 𝖬𝗇𝖽 ⁡ ( 𝔻 ) \mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D}) of monads in 𝔻 \mathbb{D} is monadic over the category of endomorphisms 𝖤𝗇𝖽 ⁡ ( 𝔻 ) \mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D}) , is cocomplete or even locally presentable. We also tackle the issue of local presentability in the dual case 𝖢𝗆𝖽 ⁡ ( 𝔻 ) \mathsf{Cmd}(\mathbb{D}) of comonads. In these results, our assumptions on the double category 𝔻 \mathbb{D} revolve around notions of colimit, in particular those of parallel and stable local colimits, as well as a notion of local presentability of a double category which has been introduced in previous work.



1.
Introduction

Double categories were introduced in the ’60s by Charles Ehresmann
[
Ehr63
]
, and have since been developed by multiple authors in various seminal works like
[
DP93
,
GP99
,
GP04
,
FPP07
,
Shu08
,
Nie12
]
.
More recently, the subject has experienced somewhat of a renaissance, with numerous authors developing a wide spectrum of double-categorical subjects, spanning also various generalisations – such as virtual double categories, enrichment in double categories and notions of double-categorical logic. A few indicative, but certainly not exhaustive, references are
[
Ale18
,
MSV22
,
Cla22
,
CLPS22
,
Ark25
,
ML25
]
.

Concerning the current work, the concept of a monad, together with its dual of comonad, have of course been of central importance in ordinary category theory and subsequently in 2-dimensional category theory since the very beginning of the subject, see e.g.
[
Str72
]
. Although it is well known that a monad in a double category, as an object, reduces to that of a monad in its corresponding (horizontal) bicategory, the move to the double-categorical notion is not at all meaningless. In fact, the extra degree of freedom afforded to the 2-cells in a double category is precisely what is needed so that the associated notion of monad morphism captures what it “morally should” in many examples, contrary to the corresponding bicategorical notion. This will also be illustrated by the examples included in the first section below.

This manuscript can thus be positioned among works that explore fundamental properties of categories of monads (previously called monoids in certain references) in double categories, like
[
Shu08
,
FGK11
,
FGK12
,
ASV24
,
GGV25
]
.
More precisely, the purpose of this work is to identify conditions on a double category
\mathbb{D}
that will imply certain desirable properties for the category
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
of monads therein, and their dual counterparts – to the extent that they hold – for the category of
\mathsf{Cmd}(\mathbb{D})
of comonads. The particular properties we are interested in concern the existence of free monads, cocompleteness, monadicity over the category of endomorphisms and local presentability for
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
, and dually the existence of free comonads, comonadicity and also local presentability for
\mathsf{Cmd}(\mathbb{D})
. The relevant conditions for the surrounding double category
\mathbb{D}
are quite natural and divide in two different sets: the one resembles a more standard bicategorical setting – namely a fibrant double category whose bicategory has sufficiently many local colimits – and applies only to the case of monads, and the other set of conditions assumes local presentability on
\mathbb{D}
itself, a concept introduced in the recent
[
ASV24
]
.

More explicitly,
3.1
,
3.8
and
3.11
establish existence of free monads, monadicity and cocompleteness of the category of monads under the first set of assumptions,
3.2
,
3.8
and
3.16
establish existence of free monads, monadicity and local presentability of the category of monads under the second set of assumptions, and
4.1
,
4.3
and
4.4
establish existence of free comonads, comonadicity and local presentability of the category of comonads – bearing in mind that local presentability does not dualize. Very broadly speaking, the first trail concerning monads extends results from
[
BCSW83
]
to the double-categorical context, whereas the other trails use methods inspired by
[
Por08
,
AR94
]
. Moreover, as an application of the fact that the category of monads is locally presentable, we obtain the known fact that
\mathcal{V}
-
\mathsf{Cat}
is locally presentable when
\mathcal{V}
is, see
[
KL01
]
.

In the process of proving these fundamental results, we make a choice, in the first
Section

2
, of illustrating key concepts such as parallel limits and colimits, stable local limits and colimits and local presentability in a number of basic examples of double categories without assuming prior familiarity with those, such as relations, spans, matrices and polynomials. In each of these cases, fibrancy – the existence of companions and conjoints that give a coherent passage from the vertical/tight to the horizontal/loose structure – allows us to employ results from the theory of (op)fibrations and relate structures from the fibres to the total categories and vice versa. Such techniques are expected to carry on such developments onto the categories of (one-sided) modules for monads, comodules for comonads as well as bimodules and bicomodules in future work.

Acknowledgements

The authors would like to thank Nathanael Arkor, Florian De Leger, Ignacio López Franco and Ross Street for helpful discussions. All authors acknowledge that this work was implemented in the framework of H.F.R.I call “3rd Call for H.F.R.I.’s Research Projects to
Support Faculty Members & Researchers” (H.F.R.I. Project Number: 23249). The second author was financially supported by the Special Account for Research Funding of the National Technical University of Athens.

2.
Double categories

In this section,
we start by recalling (fibrant) double categories together with certain results relating to fibration structures that arise. This is mostly standard material on double category theory, found for example in
[
Shu08
,
Gra19
]
. We then discuss parallel limits in double categories, local presentability in double categories as well as the categories of monads and comonads, which are the main objects of interest in this work. Some of this material is drawn from
[
ASV24
]
– which provides further particular references – and some parts are new. We also present a number of examples to illustrate the theory.

Double and fibrant double categories

We here recall certain standard definitions, mostly in order to set terminology.

Definition 2.1
.

A
(pseudo) double category

\mathbb{D}

consists of a category
\mathbb{D}_{0}
of objects and
a category
\mathbb{D}_{1}
of arrows, with identity, source and target, and composition structure
given by the functors

\mathbf{1}\colon\mathbb{D}_{0}\to\mathbb{D}_{1},\quad\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}\colon\mathbb{D}_{1}\rightrightarrows\mathbb{D}_{0},\quad\text{and}\quad\odot\colon\mathbb{D}_{1}{\times_{\mathbb{D}_{0}}}\mathbb{D}_{1}\to\mathbb{D}_{1}

respectively such that

\mathfrak{s}(1_{X})
=
\mathfrak{t}(1_{X})
=
X,\;\mathfrak{s}(M\odot N)
=
\mathfrak{s}(N),\;\mathfrak{t}(M\odot N)
=
\mathfrak{t}(M)

for all
X\in\mathrm{ob}(\mathbb{D}_{0})
and
M,N\in\mathrm{ob}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
,
equipped with natural isomorphisms

a\colon(M\odot N)\odot P\overset{\sim}{\Rightarrow}M\odot(N\odot P)
,
\ell\colon 1_{\mathfrak{s}(M)}\odot M\overset{\sim}{\Rightarrow}M
,
r\colon M\odot 1_{\mathfrak{t}(M)}\overset{\sim}{\Rightarrow}M

in
\mathbb{D}_{1}
such that

\mathfrak{t}(a),\mathfrak{s}(a),\mathfrak{t}(\ell),\mathfrak{s}(\ell),\mathfrak{t}(r),\mathfrak{s}(r)
are all
identities, and satisfying the usual coherence conditions.

The objects of
\mathbb{D}_{0}
are the
0-cells
and its morphisms are the

vertical
or
tight 1-cells

f\colon X\to Y
. The objects of
\mathbb{D}_{1}
are the
horizontal
or
loose 1-cells

A 2-cell whose vertical 1-cell components are both identity morphisms, such as the structural isomorphisms

above, is called

From any double category
\mathbb{D}
, we obtain a bicategory
\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{D})
called its
horizontal bicategory
, consisting of the 0-cells, the horizontal 1-cells and only the globular 2-cells between them.

and

the subcategories of
\mathbb{D}_{1}

Similarly,
{}^{X}\mathbb{D}^{Y}_{1}=\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{D})(X,Y)=\mathbb{D}[X,Y]
denotes the subcategory of fixed-domain and fixed-codomain horizontal 1-cells together with globular 2-cells, namely the hom-categories of its horizontal bicategory.

\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
can be equivalently written as the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}_{0}\times\mathbb{D}_{0}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}

Example 2.2
.

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
which has pullbacks, there is a double category
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathcal{C})
whose vertical category
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathcal{C})_{0}
is
\mathcal{C}
itself with horizontal 1-cells

. A 2-cell

in

making the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

commute. Horizontal composition is given by pullback, which is of course associative only up to (coherent) isomorphism, while for every object

the 2-cell

is the span of identities
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm{id}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm{id}_{X}}

Notice how the category of horizontal endo-1-cells
\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathcal{C}))
is
\mathsf{Grph}(\mathcal{C})
, the category of internal graphs in
\mathcal{C}
.

Example 2.3
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a category with pullbacks which is furthermore regular, then there is also a double category
\mathbb{R}\mathsf{el}(\mathcal{C})
whose horizontal 1-cells are relations in
\mathcal{C}
. Horizontal composition is then given by the usual composition of relations in any regular category: after performing the pullback as in
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathcal{C})
above, one takes the monic part of the (regular epi, mono)-factorization of the resulting span (viewed as a morphism into a binary product). The unit 1-cell

is the diagonal relation

Note that now a 2-cell
{}^{f}\alpha^{g}:M\Rightarrow N
can be described as a pair of morphisms
(f,g)
in
\mathcal{C}
such that we have an inclusion of relations
gMf^{\circ}\subseteq N
(or equivalently
M\subseteq g^{\circ}Nf
), since the morphism
\alpha\colon M\to N
is uniquely determined. For
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Set}
this is just the implication
(x,y)\in M\implies(f(x),g(y))\in N
.

are functors
S\colon Y\times X\to\mathcal{V}
, where
Y\times X
is viewed
as a discrete category; equivalently, these are families of objects
\{S(y,x)\}_{(y,x)\in Y\times X}
in
\mathcal{V}
,
sometimes also denoted
\{S_{y,x}\}
. The 2-morphisms
{}^{f}\alpha^{g}\colon S\Rightarrow T
are natural transformations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z\times W}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptstyle\Downarrow\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\times f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}

given by families of arrows
\alpha_{y,x}\colon S(y,x)\to T(g(y),f(x))
in
\mathcal{V}
, for all
x\in X
and
y\in Y
.

is simply the ring
R
equipped with its canonical structure as an
(R,R)
-bimodule. A 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptstyle\Downarrow\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}}

is homomorphism
\phi\colon M\to M^{\prime}
of
(S,R)
-bimodules, where
M^{\prime}
is viewed as such by restriction of scalars on the left and right along
g
and
f
respectively. In other words,
\phi
is an abelian group homomorphism which satisfies
\phi(sm)=g(s)\phi(m)
and
\phi(mr)=\phi(m)f(r)

for all
m\in M
,
r\in R
and
s\in S
.

We should mention here that
\mathbb{R}\mathsf{ing}
is a special case of the general construction
[
Shu08
]
of the double category
\mathbb{B}\mathsf{im}(\mathbb{D})
, or
\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}(\mathbb{D})
, of monads and bimodules in
\mathbb{D}
1
1

1

Of course, the bimodule construction also captures many other interesting examples of double categories. For instance, given a cocomplete monoidal closed category
\mathcal{V}
,
\mathbb{B}\mathsf{im}(\mathcal{V}\textrm{-}\mathbb{M}\mathsf{at})
is the double category
\mathcal{V}\textrm{-}\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}
of
\mathcal{V}
-enriched categories and profunctors (or distributors). Correspondingly, for a category
\mathcal{C}
with pullbacks and universal coequalizers,
\mathbb{B}\mathsf{im}(\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathcal{C}))
is the double category
\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}(\mathcal{C})
of internal categories and distributors.
, where
\mathbb{D}
is required to have
stable local
coequalizers in our terminology (
2.23
). Specifically,
\mathbb{R}\mathsf{ing}
is obtained by taking
\mathbb{D}
to be the double category
\mathbb{A}\mathsf{b}
of abelian groups; the latter is just the monoidal category
\mathsf{Ab}
considered as an one-object, only identity vertical arrow double category.

Example 2.7
.

For a locally cartesian closed category
\mathcal{C}
, the double category
\mathbb{P}\mathsf{oly}(\mathcal{C})
of polynomials over
\mathcal{C}
introduced in
[
GK13
]
, has 0-cells and vertical 1-cells the objects and arrows of
\mathcal{C}
respectively, i.e.
\mathbb{P}\mathsf{oly}(\mathcal{C})_{0}=\mathcal{C}
. Its horizontal 1-cells are
polynomials
over
\mathcal{C}
, namely diagrams of the form

I\leftarrow X\to Y\to J

in
\mathcal{C}
which induce polynomial endofunctors of
\mathcal{C}
. A 2-cell between two polynomials
I\leftarrow X\to Y\to J
and
I^{\prime}\leftarrow X^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}\to J^{\prime}
is a commutative diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{I^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{J^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

which represents a natural transformation between the induced polynomial functors. Composition of
I\leftarrow X\to Y\to J
and
J\leftarrow X^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}\to J^{\prime}
is the polynomial
I\leftarrow M\to N\to J^{\prime}
obtained by taking pullbacks and distributivity pullbacks
[
Web15
, Def. 2.2.2]
, as follows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
d.pb
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{J^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

We also note another variation, that of
\mathbb{P}\mathsf{oly}_{c}(\mathcal{C})

[
FGK11
]
which is the sub-double category of
\mathbb{P}\mathsf{oly}(\mathcal{C})
with 2-cells diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

whose middle square is a pullback. These represent only cartesian natural transformations between the induced polynomial functors
[
GK13
, 3.12]
.

More generally, we can define the double category of polynomials for any category
\mathcal{C}
with pullbacks
[
Web15
]
as long as we require polynomials to be
I\leftarrow X\to Y\to J
with
X\to Y
exponentiable.

For the remainder of this paper we shall essentially restrict to the setting of
fibrant
double categories (also called
framed bicategories

[
Shu08
]
) whose definition we recall below. One technical reason for this is to have a nice relationship between (co)limits in the categories
\mathbb{D}_{1}
,
\mathbb{D}_{0}
on one hand and the various fixed-domain or codomain 1-cell subcategories on the other. In practice, most double categories of interest satisfy this property: indeed, all the examples mentioned earlier are fibrant.

Definition 2.8
.

A
fibrant double category
is a double category
\mathbb{D}
such that the functor

(2)

\langle\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}\rangle\colon\mathbb{D}_{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{0}\times\mathbb{D}_{0}

is a fibration, or equivalently an opfibration.

This means that vertical 1-cells can be turned into horizontal 1-cells in a canonical way. Explicitly, given a vertical 1-cell
f:X\to Y
in
\mathbb{D}
,
a
companion
, resp.
conjoint
, of
f

Example 2.9
.

In the double category
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathcal{C})
, given any
f\colon X\to Y\in\mathcal{C}
, the companion and conjoint are respectively the spans

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm{id_{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm{id_{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

The same holds in
\mathbb{R}\mathsf{el}(\mathcal{C})
. In other words,
\widehat{f}
is
f
viewed as a relation (sometimes called its
graph
), while
\widecheck{f}
is precisely the opposite of the latter and is usually denoted by
f^{\circ}
.

Example 2.10
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a regular
\mathsf{Pos}
-enriched category and
f\colon X\to Y\in\mathcal{C}
, then in
\mathbb{R}\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{Idl}}(\mathcal{C})

\widehat{f}
and
\widecheck{f}
are the relations obtained as the following two comma squares:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widecheck{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

If
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Pos}
, then we have precisely
\widehat{f}=\{(x,y)\in X\times Y|f(x)\leq y\}
and
\widecheck{f}=\{(y,x)\in Y\times X|y\leq f(x)\}
.

Example 2.13
.

The double categories
\mathbb{P}\mathsf{oly}(\mathcal{C})
and
\mathbb{P}\mathsf{oly}_{c}(\mathcal{C})
are fibrant
[
GK13
]
. By similar arguments as in
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathcal{C})
, for a vertical 1-cell
f:X\to Y
, its companion and conjoint are given in both cases by

X\overset{\mathrm{id}_{X}}{\longleftarrow}X\overset{\mathrm{id}_{X}}{\longrightarrow}X\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}Y\ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ Y\overset{f}{\longleftarrow}X\overset{\mathrm{id}_{X}}{\longrightarrow}X\overset{\mathrm{id}_{X}}{\longrightarrow}X

respectively.

In what follows, we establish various adjunctions between fibrations that arise in the fibrant double setting. We here provide the statement of a general ‘fibred adjunction’ result which is used repeatedly, and follows from an inspection of the proof of
[
Bor94
, Prop. 8.4.2]
. Recall that a functor between fibrations is called
fibred
when it makes the formed triangle commute and preserves cartesian liftings.

Proposition 2.14
.

Suppose
H\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
is a fibred functor between fibrations
F\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{C}
and
G\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{C}
. Then
H
has a left adjoint
L\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
if for every
I\in\mathcal{C}
, there are ‘fiberwise’ adjunctions
L_{I}\dashv H_{I}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}

Its dual statement for a right adjoint of an opfibred functor holds as well, reducing its existence to adjunctions between the fibres. On the other hand, right adjoints for fibred functors and left adjoints for opfibred functors require extra conditions. For a relevant discussion, see e.g.
[
Vas14
, § 5.3]
.

Parallel limits and colimits

We review some basic material concerning parallel limits and colimits in double categories, see
[
ASV24
, § 3.1]
. Notably, these do not coincide with the general double-categorical (co)limits found in e.g.
[
GP99
]
, rather correspond to ‘local’ ones; the terminology was introduced by Bob Paré.

Definition 2.15
.

Let
\mathbb{D}
be a double category and
\mathcal{I}
is any small category. We say that
\mathbb{D}
has
parallel

\mathcal{I}
-(co)limits
if the categories
\mathbb{D}_{0}
,
\mathbb{D}_{1}
both have
\mathcal{I}
-(co)limits and the functors
\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}\colon\mathbb{D}_{1}\to\mathbb{D}_{0}

preserve them. If
\mathbb{D}
has parallel
\mathcal{I}
-(co)limits for any small
\mathcal{I}
, then we say that it is
parallel
(co)complete
.

Example 2.16
.

The double category
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathcal{C})
has parallel
\mathcal{I}
-(co)limits for any
\mathcal{I}
for which
\mathcal{C}
itself has
\mathcal{I}
-(co)limits. This is easy to see because
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathcal{C})_{1}
can be viewed as a category of functors into
\mathcal{C}
.

Example 2.17
.

The double category
\mathbb{R}\mathsf{el}(\mathcal{C})
has parallel
\mathcal{I}
-limits whenever
\mathcal{C}
itself has
\mathcal{I}
-limits. These are computed as in
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathcal{C})
, since the span induced between limits by a diagram of relations is itself jointly monic. It is also true that
\mathcal{C}
having
\mathcal{I}
-colimits yields parallel
\mathcal{I}
-colimits in
\mathbb{R}\mathsf{el}(\mathcal{C})
. The latter are constructed by first forming the colimit of underlying spans and then taking the image in
\mathcal{C}
of the corresponding morphism into the binary product, in order to yield a jointly monic pair.

Example 2.18
.

The double category
\mathcal{V}\textrm{-}\mathbb{M}\mathsf{at}
has parallel coproducts.
More generally, if
\mathcal{V}
has
\mathcal{I}
-colimits for some small
\mathcal{I}
, then
\mathcal{V}\textrm{-}\mathbb{M}\mathsf{at}
has parallel
\mathcal{I}
-colimits
[
ASV24
, Proposition 3.1.10]
.

Example 2.19
.

The double category
\mathbb{R}\mathsf{ing}
is parallel cocomplete. Regarding coequalizers in
\mathbb{R}\mathsf{ing}_{1}
, consider

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle\bullet

on the abelian group
R\oplus R^{\prime}
. Consider the free

-bimodule

on the abelian group
M\oplus M^{\prime}
and set

, where
V
is the sub-bimodule generated by all elements of the following forms:

\begin{cases}s\otimes m\otimes 1-1\otimes sm\otimes 1,&s\in S,m\in M\\

1\otimes m^{\prime}\otimes r^{\prime}-1\otimes m^{\prime}r^{\prime}\otimes 1,&r^{\prime}\in R^{\prime},m^{\prime}\in M^{\prime}\end{cases}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{S^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M+M^{\prime}}

which, as can be easily verified, constitute a coproduct diagram in
\mathbb{R}\mathsf{ing}_{1}
.

Example 2.20
.

Limits and colimits in
\mathbb{P}\mathsf{oly}(\mathcal{C})
have only been systematically studied in the case of
\mathbb{P}\mathsf{oly}(\mathsf{Set})
. In particular, it has been shown that
\mathbb{P}\mathsf{oly}(\mathsf{Set})_{1}
has all small limits
[
NS24
, Thm 5.33]
and colimits
[
NS24
, Thm 5.43]

Moreover, the source and target functors
\mathfrak{s,t}
are right adjoints and are thus limit-preserving so we can deduce that
\mathbb{P}\mathsf{oly}(\mathsf{Set})
is parallel complete.

Below, we recall
[
ASV24
, Prop. 3.1.11& 3.1.13]
respectively. We give a short alternative proof for the first one, essentially to recall the well-known fact that (op)fibrations can be seen to strictly preserve a choice of (co)limits when they are (co)continuous in the ordinary sense.

Proposition 2.21
.

Let
\mathbb{D}
be a fibrant double category which has parallel
\mathcal{I}
-(co)limits, for some small category
\mathcal{I}
. Then
\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
has

\mathcal{I}
-(co)limits and the inclusion
\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})\to\mathbb{D}_{1}
creates them.

Proof.

Suppose
D:\mathcal{I}\to\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
is a diagram in
\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
. Since
\mathbb{D}
has parallel
\mathcal{I}
-colimits,
\mathcal{I}\to\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{1}
has a colimit

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptstyle\Downarrow q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{s}(q_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{t}(q_{i})}
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}

We will show that
D
and
q_{i}
are in
\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
. Since
\mathbb{D}
is fibrant,
\langle\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}\rangle
is a bifibration and also projections and compositions of bifibrations are bifibrations
[
Bor94
, Prop. 8.1.12 & 13]
. Therefore, both composites

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}_{0}\times\mathbb{D}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}

are bifibrations and so they both (strictly) preserve colimits. This means that
\mathfrak{s}(q_{i})=\mathfrak{t}(q_{i})
. ∎

Proposition 2.22
.

Let
\mathbb{D}
be a fibrant double category, and
\mathbb{D}_{0}
be complete. The following are equivalent:

(2.22(i))

\mathbb{D}
is parallel complete;

(2.22(ii))

{}^{X}\mathbb{D}_{1}
and
\mathbb{D}_{1}^{Z}
are complete categories for any
X,Z\in\mathbb{D}_{0}
(and
\textrm{-}\odot\widehat{f}\colon^{Y}\mathbb{D}_{1}\to{}^{X}\mathbb{D}_{1}
and
\widecheck{g}\odot\textrm{-}\colon\mathbb{D}_{1}^{W}\to\mathbb{D}_{1}^{Z}
are continuous functors for any
f\colon X\to Y
and
g\colon Z\to W
);

(2.22(iii))

\mathbb{D}[{X},{Z}]
is a complete category for any
X,Z\in\mathbb{D}_{0}
(and
-\odot\widehat{f}\colon\mathbb{D}[{Y},{Z}]\to\mathbb{D}[{X},{Z}]
and
\widecheck{g}\odot-\colon\mathbb{D}[{X},{W}]\to\mathbb{D}[{X},{Z}]
are continuous functors).

We now recall the following terminology from
[
GGV25
, Lem. 2.4.1]
.

Definition 2.23
.

For a double category
\mathbb{D}
and a small category
\mathcal{I}
, we say that
\mathbb{D}
has
local
\mathcal{I}
-(co)limits
if
\mathbb{D}[{X},{Y}]
has
\mathcal{I}
-(co)limits for any
X,Y\in\mathbb{D}_{0}
. Such (co)limits will be called
stable
if they are preserved by the functors
M\odot-\colon\mathbb{D}[{Z},{X}]\to\mathbb{D}[{Z},{Y}]
and
-\odot M\colon\mathbb{D}[{Y},{Z}]\to\mathbb{D}[{X},{Z}]
for any
M\colon X\hbox to38pt{\vbox to9.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 18.99997pt\lower-2.46147pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-22.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -8.03 3.46 L 7.47 3.46}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.60004pt}{2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

and
Z\in\mathbb{D}_{0}
.

Remark 2.24
.

Observe that a double category with parallel (co)limits of some given type also has local colimits of that type. However, stability of such colimits is a strictly stronger requirement. In the converse direction, local (co)limits can be used to obtain parallel such (co)limits. In fact, in
2.22
the continuity conditions for
\textrm{-}\odot\widehat{f}
and
\widecheck{g}\odot\textrm{-}
can be dropped; hence, it follows that in the fibrant setting existence of (co)limits in
\mathbb{D}_{0}
together with local (co)limits in
\mathbb{D}
imply existence of parallel (co)limits in
\mathbb{D}
, see also
[
GGV25
, Lem. 2.4.2]
.

Because of this fact and in order to ease the statement of the assumptions in results throughout the paper, we make the the following convention: whenever we say that a double category
\mathbb{D}

has stable local
\mathcal{I}
-colimits
, this will include the assumption that
\mathbb{D}_{0}
has
\mathcal{I}
-colimits. Thus, having stable local colimits of a given kind will in particular imply the existence of parallel such colimits.

Example 2.25
.

The local
\mathcal{I}
-colimits in
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathcal{C})
will be stable precisely when the
\mathcal{I}
-colimits in
\mathcal{C}
are
universal
(=pullback-stable). The same remains true for
\mathbb{R}\mathsf{el}(\mathcal{C})
,
since the extra step in the construction of colimits involves taking a (regular epi, mono) factorization and the latter are also stable under pullback.

The double category
\mathcal{V}\textrm{-}\mathbb{M}\mathsf{at}
has local
\mathcal{I}
-colimits when
\mathcal{V}
has, since
\mathcal{V}\textrm{-}\mathbb{M}\mathsf{at}[X,Y]=[Y\times X,\mathcal{V}]
. It will have stable local
\mathcal{I}
-colimits if the tensor product preserves
\mathcal{I}
-colimits in each variable. In particular, if
\mathcal{V}
is cocomplete and monoidal closed, then it has all stable local colimits.

We end this subsection by discussing a notion of local presentability for fibrant double categories investigated in
[
ASV24
, § 3.4]
.

\mathbb{D}

, and of course stronger than just asking that
\mathbb{D}

2.24
). It is also clear that every locally presentable double category is parallel complete and cocomplete.

Example 2.28
.

For
\mathcal{V}
a locally presentable and monoidal category, where
\otimes
preserves filtered colimits in each variable,
\mathcal{V}\textrm{-}\mathbb{M}\mathsf{at}
is a locally presentable double category, as established in
[
ASV24
, Prop. 3.4.8]
.

Moreover,
\mathbb{R}\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{Set})
is locally presentable by
[
ASV24
, Ex. 3.4.7]
, and
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathcal{C})
is locally presentable when
\mathcal{C}
itself is locally presentable by
[
ASV24
, Ex. 3.4.8]
.

Corollary 2.29
.

[
ASV24
, Prop. 3.4.4 and 3.4.9]

Suppose that
\mathbb{D}
is a locally presentable double category. Then for every
X,Y\in\mathbb{D}_{0}
, the categories
{}^{X}\mathbb{D}_{1}
,
\mathbb{D}_{1}^{Y}
and
\mathbb{D}[{X},{Y}]
are locally presentable, as well as
\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
.

Remark 2.30
.

As remarked already in
[
ASV24
, § 3.4]
, our running notion of local presentability is not a proper 2-dimensional one. It was initially chosen with an eye towards establishing results in loc. cit., where in particular the various fibers
{}^{X}\mathbb{D}_{1}
,
\mathbb{D}_{1}^{Y}
and
\mathbb{D}[{X},{Y}]
being locally presentable was a property needed in order to establish the existence of certain adjoint functors. At the same time, this set of conditions could indeed be verified in the examples of interest. In attempting to strike a balance between these two needs, one could of course formulate variations, either weaker or stronger, depending on the circumstances and goals.

In the first direction, one could ostensibly drop the requirement that
\mathbb{D}_{1}
be locally presentable and instead directly require that all categories
{}^{X}\mathbb{D}_{1}
,
\mathbb{D}_{1}^{Y}
be locally presentable, thus producing a definition which is essentially a
fiberwise
notion of local presentability of
\mathbb{D}
. Indeed, we are often eventually working in some fiber of
\mathbb{D}
and the fibers are also generally easier to handle, while on the other hand the category
\mathbb{D}_{1}
could be “too big” to be locally presentable. However, with this weakening we clearly also lose some potentially desirable properties, such as
\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
being locally presentable.

In the other direction, one could consider a definition which is more along the lines of postulating that
\mathbb{D}
is an internal category in an appropriate category of locally presentable categories. Explicitly, this would additionally involve the requirements that the unit
1\colon\mathbb{D}_{0}\to\mathbb{D}_{1}
and horizontal composition
-\odot-\colon\mathbb{D}_{1}\times_{\mathbb{D}_{0}}\mathbb{D}_{1}\to\mathbb{D}_{1}
preserve filtered colimits. Indeed, we will have to impose a weaker version of these in our main
3.16
and
4.4
below, where horizontal composition is assumed to preserve filtered colimits merely as a functor
-\odot-\colon\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})\to\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
. In passing, let us note that it is not too hard to see that the stronger definition would indeed imply both this latter condition as well as the conditions in
2.26
.

For our current purposes, we keep the formulation of
2.26
for local presentability of double categories, asking for different conditions when they are needed and not implied (e.g.
3.14
).

2.29
establishes that the category of horizontal endo-1-cells is locally presentable, whereas one of the main goals of this work is to establish that moreover, the categories of monads and comonads are also locally presentable. We now turn to these categories.

Monads and comonads

Let us now recall the basic structures that this work investigates, namely monads and comonads in double categories.

{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}Z
equipped with globular 2-morphisms
\delta\colon C\Rightarrow C\odot C
and
\epsilon\colon C\Rightarrow 1_{U}

satisfying the usual coassociativity and counit axioms.
A
comonad morphism
is a 2-morphism
{}^{f}\alpha^{f}\colon C\Rightarrow D

respecting the comultiplications and counits.

We obtain a category
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
, which is in fact the vertical category of a double category of monads introduced in

[
FGK11
, Def. 2.4]
, as well as a category
\mathsf{Cmd}(\mathbb{D})
. These are both subcategories of
\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
since vertical boundary maps of (co)monad maps coincide.

Example 2.32
.

It is well-known that a monad in
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathcal{C})
is a category internal to
\mathcal{C}
. It consists of a horizontal endo-1-cell, i.e. a span of the form
X_{0}\overset{s}{\leftarrow}X_{1}\overset{t}{\to}X_{0}
;
X_{0}
will be the intended object of objects and
X_{1}
the intended object of arrows; the left and right arrows
s
and
t
are the source and target respectively. Furthermore, it comes equipped with a composition
\mu:X_{1}\times_{X_{0}}X_{1}\to X_{1}
for compatible arrows which is assocative and a unit
1:X_{0}\to X_{1}
which assigns the identity arrow to every object. The axioms for the unit imply that it is a neutral element for the composition. Finally, a monad morphism then becomes precisely an internal functor, so that
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathcal{C}))
is
\mathsf{Cat}(\mathcal{C})
.

A comonad on the other hand is just an arrow in
\mathcal{C}
. Indeed, for a horizontal endo-1-cell
C:X\overset{f}{\leftarrow}Y\overset{g}{\to}X
, the existence of a counit
\varepsilon:C\Rightarrow 1_{X}
implies that
f=g=\varepsilon
while the existence of a comultiplication imply that
\Delta
is just the diagonal; the axioms then become trivial equations.

Example 2.33
.

A monad in
\mathbb{R}\mathsf{el}(\mathcal{C})
is a reflexive and transitive internal relation, i.e. an internal preorder. Indeed, the existence of 2-cells
\eta:1_{X}\to R
and
\mu:R\odot R\to R
describes the inclusions
\Delta_{X}\subseteq R
and
RR\subseteq R
, respectively. In addition, a monad morphism is clearly just an order preserving morphism between internal preorders, so that
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{R}\mathsf{el}(\mathcal{C}))
is
\mathsf{Preord}(\mathcal{C})
.

On the other hand, a comonad

Example 2.34
.

A monad in

is a category enriched in

: it is a

-matrix

namely a family
\{A(x^{\prime},x)\}
of objects in
\mathcal{V}
, together with morphisms
A(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})\otimes A(x,x^{\prime})\to A(x,x^{\prime\prime})
and

in
\mathcal{V}

\mathcal{V}\textrm{-}\mathbb{M}\mathsf{at}
is precisely a
\mathcal{V}
-functor, capturing
\mathcal{V}\textrm{-}\mathsf{Cat}
as
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathcal{V}\textrm{-}\mathbb{M}\mathsf{at})
. Dually, the category of comonads in matrices is
\mathcal{V}
-
\mathsf{Cocat}
of
\mathcal{V}
-

and
\mathcal{V}
-

: for more details, see
[
Vas19
]
.

Example 2.35
.

\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{R}\mathsf{ing})
is a global category
\mathsf{Alg}
of algebras over all rings. In more detail, a monad is an
(R,R)
-bimodule
M
for some ring
R
together with two
(R,R)
-bimodule homomorphisms
\mu:M\otimes_{R}M\to M
and
\eta:R\to M
. The axioms for
\mu
and
\eta
imply that
\mu
is an associative binary operation on
M
and
\eta(1_{R})
is a unit. Dually,
\mathsf{Cmd}(\mathbb{R}\mathsf{ing})
is a global category of coalgebras over all rings.

Example 2.36
.

For the case where
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Set}
, the slice categories of
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{P}\mathsf{oly}(\mathsf{Set}))
over some polynomial monad
P
give
P
-multicategories
[
GK13
, Cor. 5.17]
. In particular, the slice over the monoid monad
M
is the category of coloured non-symmetric operads and the slice over the identity monad give small categories.

In the fibrant setting, categories of horizontal endo-1-cells, monads and comonads obtain certain (op)fibration structures over
\mathbb{D}_{0}
, see
[
FGK11
, Prop. 3.3]
or

[
ASV24
, Prop. 3.3.4]
.

Proposition 2.37
.

Suppose
\mathbb{D}
is a fibrant double category. Then
\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
is bifibred over
\mathbb{D}_{0}
,
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
is fibred over
\mathbb{D}_{0}
and
\mathsf{Cmd}(\mathbb{D})
is opfibred over
\mathbb{D}_{0}
.

Proof.

We briefly recall the related structures. For
\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
, the fiber above some
X\in\mathbb{D}_{0}
is the endo-hom category
\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{D})(X,X)=\mathbb{D}[{X},{X}]
and for any vertical arrow
f:X\to Y
, the reindexing functors are

(5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}[{X},{X}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}[{Y},{Y}].}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{f}\odot\,-\,\odot\check{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{f}\odot\,-\,\odot\hat{f}}

For
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
and
\mathsf{Cmd}(\mathbb{D})
,
the fiber over some
X\in\mathbb{D}_{0}
is the category of (co)monoids over the monoidal category
(\mathbb{D}[{X},{X}],\odot,1_{X})
with the same reindexing functors, which can be seen to restrict to those categories accordingly.
∎

Very similarly to
2.21
, parallel limits in fibrant double categories induce limits in
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
and dually for
\mathsf{Cmd}(\mathbb{D})
, see
[
ASV24
, Prop. 3.3.6]
.

Proposition 2.38
.

If a fibrant double category
\mathbb{D}
has parallel
\mathcal{I}
-limits, then the category
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
has all
\mathcal{I}
-limits and the inclusion
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})\to\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
creates them. Dually, if
\mathbb{D}
has parallel
\mathcal{I}
-colimits, then the category
\mathsf{Cmd}(\mathbb{D})
has
\mathcal{I}
-colimits and the inclusion
\mathsf{Cmd}(\mathbb{D})\to\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
creates them.

Remark 2.39
.

It is known (e.g.
[
ASV24
, Cor. 2.2.3]
) that for any fibration, the inclusion of a fibre into the total category preserves any
connected
limit that the fibration preserves, and dually for colimits in opfibrations. This is worth mentioning, because it clarifies that in general, limits in the fibre are not the same as limits in the total category even in the the case of ‘fibred complete’ fibrations. In the above case, when
\mathbb{D}
has parallel connected limits and colimits, the inclusion of the fiber
\mathbb{D}[{X},{X}]\to\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
in the total category of the (op)fibration
\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})\to\mathbb{D}_{0}
preserves all connected limits and colimits, and similarly for monads and comonads. The same holds under the stronger assumption (
2.24
) of stable local connected limits and colimits.

3.
Monads

In this section, we study fundamental properties of the category of monads in a double category. Firstly, we show that
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
is monadic over the category of endomorphisms
\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
under
suitable hypotheses on the double category
\mathbb{D}
–
3.7
. This can be seen as a generalization of the monadicity
of
\mathcal{V}\textrm{-}\mathsf{Cat}
over
\mathcal{V}\textrm{-}\mathsf{Grph}
for a (suitable) monoidal category
\mathcal{V}
(
[
Wol74
]
), or the similar and more general result concerning
\mathcal{W}\textrm{-}\mathsf{Cat}
over a bicategory
\mathcal{W}
(
[
BCSW83
]
). Second, we prove that
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
is cocomplete, again under suitable assumptions on
\mathbb{D}
. In fact, part of our reasoning establishing this result faithfully adapts arguments of
[
BCSW83
]
, but providing the necessary adaptations to the double-categorical context. Finally, we give conditions under which the category of monads
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
is locally presentable –
3.16
.

The first step, namely the existence of a free monad on an endomorphism in a double category, is based on the standard ‘free monoid construction’ in the setting of a monoidal category; see
[
Lac10
]
for a general discussion of relevant conditions.
We briefly recall one version that suits our purposes: if
\mathcal{V}
is a monoidal category with countable coproducts which are preserved by
\otimes
in each variable, then the forgetful functor
\mathsf{Mon}(\mathcal{V})\to\mathcal{V}
has a
left adjoint that maps any
A\in\mathcal{V}
to the “geometric series”
\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}A^{\otimes n}
.

Proposition 3.1
.

Suppose that
\mathbb{D}
is a fibrant double category with stable local countable coproducts. Then the
forgetful functor
U\colon\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})\to\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
has a left adjoint.

Proof.

Since
\mathbb{D}
is fibrant, the forgetful functor
U\colon\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})\to\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
is a fibred functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}

between the fibrations
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})\to\mathbb{D}_{0}
and
\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})\to\mathbb{D}_{0}
.
Indeed, the triangle commutes and the cartesian liftings for both fibrations are essentially the same, see
2.37
.
Thus, by
2.14
, for
U
to have a left adjoint it suffices to show that for every
X\in\mathbb{D}_{0}
, the restriction

U_{X}\colon\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})_{X}\to\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})_{X}
has a left adjoint.

Indeed, recall that the fiber
\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})_{X}=\mathbb{D}[{X},{X}]
is a monoidal category with tensor product given by the horizontal
composition
\odot
of
\mathbb{D}
. Moreover, the functor
U_{X}\colon\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})_{X}\to\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})_{X}
is precisely the forgetful
functor
\mathsf{Mon}(\mathbb{D}[{X},{X}])\to\mathbb{D}[{X},{X}]
. We know that
\mathbb{D}[{X},{X}]
has countable coproducts which are preserved by tensoring in each variable by assumption (see
2.23
).
Thus, by the preceding discussion we deduce that under these conditions,
U_{X}
has a left adjoint and the result is complete..
∎

Notice that based in the previous free monoid description, if

\mathbb{D}
is a double category as in the above proposition, then we see that the left adjoint
F\colon\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})\to\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
will map any

{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-22.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.1597pt}{0.87154pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

}\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{}{}{{}}

}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{

to the geometric series
\sum\limits_{n}{}_{X}G^{\odot n}
, where
\sum_{X}
denotes the coproduct in the
category
\mathbb{D}[{X},{X}]
. Note that this coproduct is generally not the same as the coproduct in
\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
(or
\mathbb{D}_{1}
).

As was discussed earlier, the existence of free monoids in the context of a monoidal category
\mathcal{V}
can be obtained via different means, depending on the surrounding assumptions on
\mathcal{V}
itself. Accordingly, as is clear from the above proof, one could formulate corresponding different sets of conditions on a double category
\mathbb{D}
that would guarantee the existence of free monads therein. We record here one such variation concerning the context of a locally presentable double category (
2.26
).

Proposition 3.2
.

Suppose that
\mathbb{D}
is a locally presentable double category. Then the forgetful functor
U\colon\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})\to\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
has a left adjoint.

Proof.

In the exact same manner to the above proof, in the underlying fibrant setting the problem is reduced to proving the existence of fibrewise adjoints, namely adjoints to
U_{X}\colon\mathsf{Mon}(\mathbb{D}[{X},{X}])\to\mathbb{D}[{X},{X}]
. Since
\mathbb{D}
is locally presentable, by
2.29
each fiber
\mathbb{D}[{X},{X}]
is locally presentable itself and moreover, by
2.27
,
\mathbb{D}[{X},{X}]
has filtered colimits which are preserved by ‘tensoring’ in each variable. Thus,
[
Por08
, § 2.6]
2
2

2

Although
[
Por08
]
requires that the base monoidal category is symmetric, an inspection of the proofs shows that symmetry can be replaced with the tensor preserving filtered colimits in both entries, rather than in one.
can be applied to yield the required left adjoint.

∎

Remark 3.3
.

We should note here that questions concerning the existence of free monads in double categories have also been considered in
[
FGK11
,
FGK12
]
. However, the approach therein differs from ours in at least a couple of aspects.

First of all, the authors of loc. cit. do not investigate conditions on the double category
\mathbb{D}
that would directly imply the existence of free monads in
\mathbb{D}
. Rather, they provide conditions under which, knowing that the horizontal bicategory
\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{D})
admits free monads, one can deduce the same for
\mathbb{D}
. Second, their notion of “free monad” is a 2-dimensional one, which is to say that it must satisfy a stronger universal property than our ordinary one. In particular, they work with a double category
\mathbb{M}\mathsf{nd}(\mathbb{D})
of monads in
\mathbb{D}
, whose vertical category is precisely our ordinary category of monads
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
.

Example 3.4
.

Specializing
3.1
to the double category
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathcal{C})
whose monads are internal categories as per
2.32
, we have that when
\mathcal{C}
has (pullbacks and) universal countable coproducts, then the forgetful functor
\mathsf{Cat}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathsf{Grph}(\mathcal{C})
from internal categories to internal graphs in
\mathcal{C}
has a left adjoint. Alternatively, going via
3.2
, it can be shown that said left adjoint exists as well when
\mathcal{C}
is locally presentable, see
2.28
.

Example 3.5
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a regular category which has universal countable coproducts. Then
\mathbb{R}\mathsf{el}(\mathcal{C})
has stable local countable coproducts and so
3.1
applies.
It is easy to see that in this case the left adjoint forms the usual “reflexive-transitive hull” of any given relation

.

Example 3.6
.

For
\mathcal{V}\textrm{-}\mathbb{M}\mathsf{at}
,
3.1
immediately applies to yield a left adjoint to
\mathcal{V}\textrm{-}\mathsf{Cat}\to\mathcal{V}\textrm{-}\mathsf{Grph}
, since arbitrary coproducts stable under tensoring with a fixed variable are already required in
\mathcal{V}
in order to define the composition in
\mathcal{V}\textrm{-}\mathbb{M}\mathsf{at}
(
2.5
).
In the special case when
\mathcal{V}
is symmetric monoidal closed, this recovers a result originally due to H. Wolff
[
Wol74
, Corollary 2.3]
.

As in the case of monoids in a monoidal category, we have that
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
is monadic over
\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
as soon as free monads exist.

Theorem 3.7
.

Let
\mathbb{D}
be a fibrant double category such that the forgetful functor
U\colon\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})\to\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
has a left adjoint. Then
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
is monadic over
\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
.

Proof.

We apply Beck’s monadicity theorem.
U\colon\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})\to\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
has a left adjoint by hypothesis, and it can be verified to reflect isomorphisms.
Lastly, if a pair or parallel monad maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptstyle\Downarrow\alpha,\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}

is such that
U\alpha_{f},U\beta_{g}
have a split coequalizer

\gamma_{e}\colon N_{Y}\Rightarrow E_{Z}
in
\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
, then we can give
E
a monad structure that makes the same diagram a coequalizer in
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
.

For the multiplication, applying
-\odot-
to the above absolute colimit we get a (split) coequalizer
\gamma\odot\gamma

of
\alpha\odot\alpha,\beta\odot\beta
in
\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
. But
\gamma\circ\mu_{N}
also coequalizes this pair via

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\odot M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N\odot N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\odot E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\odot\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta\odot\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma\odot\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\mu_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

and therefore exists a unique 2-cell
\mu_{E}\colon E\odot E\Rightarrow E
that commutes with the appropriate maps, and is moreover globular due to the vertical part of said commutativity – since
e
is the coequalizer of
f,g
in
\mathbb{D}_{0}
thus epic. To prove that
\mu_{E}
is associative,
we verify that
\mu_{E}\circ(1_{E}\odot\mu_{E})\circ(\gamma\odot\gamma\odot\gamma)=\mu_{E}\circ(\mu_{E}\odot 1_{E})\circ(\gamma\odot\gamma\odot\gamma)
and use the fact that
\gamma\odot\gamma\odot\gamma
is epic.

For the unit, applying
1_{(-)}\circ\mathfrak{s}
on the original split coequalizer yields a split coequalizer
1_{e}
of
1_{f},1_{g}
in
\mathbb{D}_{1}
– where recall that for example,
1_{e}
is the 2-map
1_{Y}\Rightarrow 1_{Z}
with vertical boundaries
e
. With similar arguments as before,
\gamma\circ\eta_{N}
also coequalizes
1_{f},1_{g}
via

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\eta_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

thus obtaining a unique 2-cell
\eta_{E}\colon 1_{E}\Rightarrow E
which, as before, commutes with the appropriate maps and is furthermore globular. For the left unitality of
\eta_{E}
, we
verify that
\mu_{E}\circ(\eta_{E}\odot\mathrm{id}_{E})\circ(1_{e}\odot\gamma)=\ell_{E}\circ(1_{e}\odot\gamma)
and use the fact that
1_{e}\odot\gamma
is epic. Similarily for the right unitality.

By their construction,
\mu_{E}
and
\eta_{E}
make
E
into a monad and
\gamma_{e}
a monad morphism, and then it can be seen that the original coequalizer remains such in
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
.
∎

In particular, we have now isolated conditions on a double category under which the category of monads is monadic over the category of endomorphisms.

Corollary 3.8
.

Let
\mathbb{D}
be a fibrant double category which satisfies either one of the following conditions:

(1)

\mathbb{D}
has stable local countable coproducts.

(2)

\mathbb{D}
is locally presentable.

Then
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
is monadic over
\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
.

We next want to tackle the question of cocompleteness of
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
, for which purpose it will suffice in our context to construct coequalizers. To accomplish this, we directly adapt the corresponding results in
[
BCSW83
]
, specifically Lemma 4 and Proposition 5 therein.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
\mathbb{D}
be a fibrant double category which has stable local coequalizers. Suppose that
\phi,\psi\colon A\to B

are morphisms in
\mathsf{Mon}(\mathbb{D}[{X},{X}],\odot,1_{X})
for an
X\in\mathbb{D}_{0}
, and let
\chi\colon B\to C
be their coequalizer in
\mathbb{D}[{X},{X}]
. Then
the following are equivalent:

(1)

C has a monad structure such that
\chi
is a (globular) monad morphism.

(2)

The morphism
\chi\mu_{B}\colon B\odot B\to C
coequalizes both pairs of morphisms
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\odot B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\odot B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi\odot\mathrm{id}_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi\odot\mathrm{id}_{B}}
and
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\odot A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\odot B.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{B}\odot\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{B}\odot\psi}

Furthermore, in this case
\chi
is the coequalizer of
(\phi,\psi)
in
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
.

In particular, if the initial pair
(\phi,\psi)
has a common splitting in

\mathbb{D}[{X},{X}]
, condition (2) is satisfied
3
3

3

We thank Ross Street for clarifying this final part of the statement.
.

Proof.

We will make use of the following
3\times 3
diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\odot A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\odot B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\odot C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\odot A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\odot B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\odot C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\odot A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\odot B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\odot C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{A}\odot\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{A}\odot\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi\odot\mathrm{id}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi\odot\mathrm{id}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{A}\odot\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi\odot\mathrm{id}_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi\odot\mathrm{id}_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi\odot\mathrm{id}_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi\odot\mathrm{id}_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{B}\odot\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{B}\odot\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi\odot\mathrm{id}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{B}\odot\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi\odot\mathrm{id}_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{C}\odot\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{C}\odot\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{C}\odot\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi\odot\mathrm{id}_{C}}

Observe that by our assumptions on
\odot
, all rows and columns are coequalizer diagrams, both in
\mathbb{D}[{X},{X}]
and
\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
. The former is included in
2.23

of stable local coequalizers and the latter since coequalizers are connected colimits, hence the inclusion
\mathbb{D}[{X},{X}]\to\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
preserves them by
2.39
.

(1)
\implies
(2):
If
C
is a monad and
\chi
is a monad morphism, then we must have
\mu_{C}(\chi\odot\chi)=\chi\mu_{B}
. This equality can be written as

\mu_{C}(\mathrm{id}_{C}\odot\chi)(\chi\odot\mathrm{id}_{B})=\chi\mu_{B}
and similarly as
\mu_{C}(\chi\odot\mathrm{id}_{C})(\mathrm{id}_{B}\odot\chi)=\chi\mu_{B}
, using the interchange law due to globularity of
\chi
. From these, by precomposing with both indicated pairs, it follows immediately that
\chi\mu_{B}
coequalizes them.

(2)
\implies
(1):
First, by the coequalizer property of the second row, there is a unique
\xi\colon B\odot C\to C\in\mathbb{D}[{X},{X}]

such that
\xi(\mathrm{id}_{B}\odot\chi)=\chi\mu_{B}
. Now the equality
\chi\mu_{B}(\phi\odot\mathrm{id}_{B})=\chi\mu_{B}(\psi\odot\mathrm{id}_{B})
, which we have by
assumption, can be rewritten as
\xi(\mathrm{id}_{B}\odot\chi)(\phi\odot\mathrm{id}_{B})=\xi(\mathrm{id}_{B}\odot\chi)(\psi\odot\mathrm{id}_{B})
and then further as

\xi(\phi\odot\mathrm{id}_{C})(\mathrm{id}_{A}\odot\chi)=\xi(\psi\odot\mathrm{id}_{C})(\mathrm{id}_{A}\odot\chi)
. But notice that
\mathrm{id}_{A}\odot\chi
is an epimorphism in
\mathbb{D}[{X},{X}]
since
A\odot-
preserves coequalizers by assumption, so that we can deduce that
\xi(\phi\odot\mathrm{id}_{C})=\xi(\psi\odot\mathrm{id}_{C})
. Now using the coequalizer property of the third
column,
(\exists!\mu_{C}\colon C\odot C\to C\in\mathbb{D}[{X},{X}])\mu_{C}(\chi\odot\mathrm{id}_{C})=\xi
. We also define
\eta_{C}\coloneqq\chi\eta_{B}
and
claim that
(C,\mu_{C},\eta_{C})
is a monad in
\mathbb{D}
.

Note that the proposed definition of
\eta_{C}
is forced upon us if
\chi
is to be a monad morphism, as in that case we must have

\chi\eta_{B}=\eta_{C}1_{\mathrm{id}_{X}}=\eta_{C}\mathrm{id}_{1_{X}}=\eta_{C}
. We also have

\mu_{C}(\chi\odot\chi)=\mu_{C}(\chi\odot\mathrm{id}_{C})(\mathrm{id}_{B}\odot\chi)=\xi(\mathrm{id}_{B}\odot\chi)=\chi\mu_{B}
. So
\chi
will indeed be a monad morphism, as
soon as we show that
C
is a monad.

For one of the unit axioms for
C
, we argue as follows:

\displaystyle\mu_{C}(\eta_{C}\odot\mathrm{id}_{C})(\mathrm{id}_{1_{X}}\odot\chi)

\displaystyle=\mu_{C}((\chi\mu_{B})\odot\mathrm{id}_{C})(\mathrm{id}_{1_{X}}\odot\chi)=\mu_{C}(\chi\odot\mathrm{id}_{C})(\eta_{B}\odot\mathrm{id}_{C})(\mathrm{id}_{1_{X}}\odot\chi)

\displaystyle=\xi(\mathrm{id}_{B}\odot\chi)(\eta_{B}\odot\mathrm{id}_{B})=\chi\mu_{B}(\eta_{B}\odot\mathrm{id}_{B})=\chi\lambda_{B}=\lambda_{C}(\mathrm{id}_{1_{X}}\odot\chi)

and since
\mathrm{id}_{1_{X}}\odot\chi
is an epimorphism, we deduce that

\mu_{C}(\eta_{C}\odot\mathrm{id}_{C})=\lambda_{C}
; similarly for the other unit axiom.
Finally, for the associativity axiom we argue as follows:

\displaystyle\mu_{C}(\mathrm{id}_{C}\odot\mu_{C})(\chi\odot\chi\odot\chi)

\displaystyle=\mu_{C}(\mathrm{id}_{C}\odot\mu_{C})(\chi\odot\mathrm{id}_{C^{2}})(\mathrm{id}_{B}\odot\chi\odot\chi)

\displaystyle=\mu_{C}(\chi\odot\mathrm{id}_{C})(\mathrm{id}_{B}\odot\mu_{C})(\mathrm{id}_{B}\odot\chi\odot\chi)

\displaystyle=\xi(\mathrm{id}_{B}\odot\mu_{C})(\mathrm{id}_{B}\odot\chi\odot\chi)=\xi(\mathrm{id}_{B}\odot\mu_{C}(\chi\odot\chi))

\displaystyle=\xi(\mathrm{id}_{B}\odot\chi\mu_{B})=\xi(\mathrm{id}_{B}\odot\chi)(\mathrm{id}_{B}\odot\mu_{B})=\chi\mu_{B}(\mathrm{id}_{B}\odot\mu_{B})

\displaystyle=\chi\mu_{B}(\mu_{B}\odot\mathrm{id}_{B})=\mu_{C}(\chi\odot\chi)(\mu_{B}\odot\mathrm{id}_{B})

\displaystyle=\mu_{C}(\chi\mu_{B}\odot\chi)=\mu_{C}(\mu_{C}(\chi\odot\chi)\odot\chi)

\displaystyle=\mu_{C}(\mu_{C}\odot\mathrm{id}_{C})(\chi\odot\chi\odot\mathrm{id}_{C})(\mathrm{id}_{B^{2}}\odot\chi)

\displaystyle=\mu_{C}(\mu_{C}\odot\mathrm{id}_{C})(\chi\odot\chi\odot\chi)

and again observe that
\chi\odot\chi\odot\chi
is an epimorphism by the assumptions on
\odot
.

In order to check that
C
remains the coequalizer in
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
, suppose that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptstyle\Downarrow\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}

is a morphism in
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
such that
\beta\phi=\beta\psi
. By the coequalizer property in
\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
there is a unique
\gamma\colon C\to D\in\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
such that
\gamma\chi=\beta
. It is then easy to verify that
\gamma
is a monad morphism, using that
\beta
is such and that
\chi\odot\chi
is an epimorphism.

Finally, assume there exists a splitting namely
\sigma\colon B\to A\in\mathbb{D}[{X},{X}]
such that
\phi\sigma=\mathrm{id}_{B}=\psi\sigma
. Then

\displaystyle\chi\mu_{B}(\phi\odot\mathrm{id}_{B})

\displaystyle=\chi\mu_{B}(\phi\odot(\phi\sigma))=\chi\mu_{B}(\phi\odot\phi)(\mathrm{id}_{A}\odot\sigma)

\displaystyle=\chi\phi\mu_{A}(\mathrm{id}_{A}\odot\sigma)=\chi\psi\mu_{A}(\mathrm{id}_{A}\odot\sigma)

\displaystyle=\chi\mu_{B}(\psi\odot\psi)(\mathrm{id}_{A}\odot\sigma)=\chi\mu_{B}(\psi\odot(\psi\sigma))

\displaystyle=\chi\mu_{B}(\psi\odot\mathrm{id}_{B})

A similar calculation yields
\chi\mu_{B}(\mathrm{id}_{B}\odot\phi)=\chi\mu_{B}(\mathrm{id}_{B}\odot\psi)
, so condition (2) indeed holds.
∎

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
\mathbb{D}
be a fibrant double category with stable local colimits. Then the category
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
has all
coequalizers.

Proof.

Throughout the proof,
U
will denote the forgetful functor
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})\to\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
and
F\colon\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})\to\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
will
be its left adjoint, whose existence was established in
3.1
under these assumptions. The counit of this adjunction will be denoted by

\epsilon
. Observe that, since the adjoint
F
was established by an appeal to
2.14
, by an inspection of the proof we also have that the components of

\epsilon
are globular morphisms.

Consider a pair of monad morphisms in
\mathbb{D}
as follows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptstyle\Downarrow\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptstyle\Downarrow\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}

Then consider the coequalizers
\gamma
and
\delta
in
\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
of the pairs
(U\phi,U\psi)
and
(UFU\phi,UFU\psi)
respectively

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UB}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptstyle\Downarrow\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UFUB}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptstyle\Downarrow\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}

We will use the free image of those two coequalizers in order to construct the coequalizer
E
of
\phi
and
\psi
in
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
. In the process, we will make use of the above lemma that, however, refers only to
globular
monad maps.

We should comment here on why we can assume that
\gamma
and
\delta
have the same vertical components. First, recall that having stable local colimits implies the existence of parallel colimits by
2.24
. Now, as in the proof of
2.21
, not only does
\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})\to\mathbb{D}_{0}
preserve colimits, namely the vertical component of both 2-cells must be a coequalizer for
(f,g)
in
\mathbb{D}_{0}
, but also it can be thought of strictly doing so due to the opfibration structure of source/target, hence we can use the same
q
.

We now obtain the following commutative diagram in
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
. In more detail, the top two rows are coequalizers in
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
because
F
is a left adjoint, and
\xi
and
\zeta
are induced by the
coequalizer property of
F\delta
due to the commutativities involving the pairs

(\epsilon_{FUA},\epsilon_{FUB})
and
(FU\epsilon_{A},FU\epsilon_{B})
respectively.
Moreover, the first two columns lie in

\mathsf{Mon}(\mathbb{D}[{X},{X}],\odot,1_{X})
and
\mathsf{Mon}(\mathbb{D}[{Y},{Y}],\odot,1_{Y})
respectively, and are in fact the canonical presentations of
A
and
B
as
Eilenberg-Moore algebras in these monadic categories over
\mathbb{D}[{X},{X}]
and
\mathbb{D}[{X},{X}]
respectively. In particular, these
coequalizers are preserved by
U
.

(6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUFUA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUFUB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FD}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FC}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUFU\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUFU\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{FUA}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FU\epsilon_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{FUB}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FU\epsilon_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FU\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FU\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

We also observe that the morphisms
\xi,\zeta
are globular. To see this, suppose that the vertical component of
\xi
is
\xi_{0}\colon Q\to Q\in\mathbb{D}_{0}
. Since
\xi\circ F\delta=F\gamma\circ\epsilon_{FUB}
and
\epsilon_{FUB}
is itself globular, taking vertical components yields

\xi_{0}q=q
. But
q
is a coequalizer in
\mathbb{D}_{0}
, so in particular an epimorphism, whence we deduce that
\xi_{0}=\mathrm{id}_{Q}
. A similar argument
applies to
\zeta
, this time using that
FU\epsilon_{B}
is globular.

Applying the forgetful functor
U
to the above diagram and considering the coequalizer
E
of
U\zeta
and
U\xi
, we have the following diagram in
\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
, where the morphism
\theta
is induced by
the coequalizer property of the second column.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UFUFUA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UFUFUB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UFD}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UFUA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UFUB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UFC}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UFUFU\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UFUFU\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\epsilon_{FUA}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UFU\epsilon_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\epsilon_{FUB}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UFU\epsilon_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UF\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UFU\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UFU\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\epsilon_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\epsilon_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UF\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}

We will now show that
E
is the coequalizer of the original morphisms
\phi
and
\psi
, namely that it obtains a monad structure. For that, we will apply
3.9
to the globular
\zeta
and
\xi
, by showing that they have a common splitting in
\mathbb{D}[{X},{X}]
. First of all, notice that the pairs of morphisms in the first two columns above both have globular common splittings in
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
, given respectively by
F\eta_{UA}
and
F\eta_{UB}
. Moreover, by naturality of
\eta
we have

\displaystyle F\delta\circ F\eta_{UB}\circ FU\phi

\displaystyle=F(\delta\circ\eta_{UB}\circ U\phi)=F(\delta\circ UFU\phi\circ\eta_{UA})=F(\delta\circ UFU\psi\circ\eta_{UA})=F(\delta\circ\eta_{UB}\circ U\psi)

\displaystyle=F\delta\circ F\eta_{UB}\circ FU\psi

By the coequalizer property of
FC
this implies the existence of a unique
\sigma\colon FC\to FD\in\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
such that
\sigma\circ F\gamma=F\delta\circ F\eta_{UB}
. Then we have that

\xi\circ\sigma\circ F\gamma=\xi\circ F\delta\circ F\eta_{UB}=F\gamma\circ\epsilon_{FUB}\circ F\eta_{UB}=F\gamma\circ\mathrm{id}_{FUB}=F\gamma

\zeta\circ\sigma\circ F\gamma=\zeta\circ F\delta\circ F\eta_{UB}=F\gamma\circ FU\epsilon_{B}\circ F\eta_{UB}=F\gamma\circ\mathrm{id}_{FUB}=F\gamma

and because
F\gamma
is an epimorphism in
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
we conclude that
\xi\sigma=\mathrm{id}_{FC}=\zeta\sigma
, hence
\sigma
is a common splitting of
\zeta
and
\xi
also in
\mathbb{D}[{X},{X}]
.

As a result,
3.9
applies and endows
E
with a monad structure such that
e
is the coequalizer of
\xi,\zeta
in
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
. Going back to
Eq.

6
in
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
, which is now completed with
E
, we have that all three columns and the first two rows are coequalizers. Thus, it follows that the bottom row is also a coequalizer in
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
.
∎

By well-known results of Linton
[
Lin69
]
, a category of Eilenberg-Moore algebras over a cocomplete base category is itself cocomplete as
soon as it has (reflexive) coequalizers. Hence, we immediately deduce the following.

Corollary 3.11
.

Let
\mathbb{D}
be a fibrant double category which has stable local colimits. Then the global category of monads

\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
is cocomplete.

Proof.

By
2.21
, under these assumptions
4
4

4

Recall that due to our terminology convention of
2.24
, the existence of stable local colimits implies the existence of parallel colimits.

\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
is cocomplete, and the monadic
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
has all coequalizers by
3.10
.
∎

Example 3.12
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a category with pullbacks and universal small colimits, then
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathcal{C})
has stable local colimits. So for such a
\mathcal{C}
, by
3.7
and
3.11
we have the well-known fact that the category of internal categories
\mathsf{Cat}(\mathcal{C})
is cocomplete and monadic over the category of internal graphs
\mathsf{Grph}(\mathcal{C})
.

Example 3.13
.

Consider a monoidal category
\mathcal{V}
which is cocomplete and such that colimits are preserved by
\otimes
in each variable. Then
\mathcal{V}\textrm{-}\mathbb{M}\mathsf{at}
has stable local colimits, so the preceding two results again apply to yield that
\mathcal{V}\textrm{-}\mathsf{Cat}
is cocomplete and monadic over
\mathcal{V}\textrm{-}\mathsf{Grph}
. Once more, in the special case when
\mathcal{V}
is symmetric monoidal closed, these recover results of H. Wolff
[
Wol74
, Theorem 2.13, Corollary 2.14]
.

Before moving on to discussing local presentability of the category
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
, we record also the following general observation concerning colimits in the category of monads. This gives a different type of assumptions on
\mathbb{D}
that would ensure the existence of a particular type of colimits in
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
and should be compared to the discussion in
2.30
(regarding preservation of filtered colimits).

Proposition 3.14
.

If a fibrant double category
\mathbb{D}

.

is a cocone in

of the initial diagram

. To show that

is colimiting therein, take another cocone

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}Y,\mu_{B},\eta_{B}),\phi_{i})
, which constitutes a cocone in
\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
as well. Therefore there is some 2-cell
\phi:A\Rightarrow B
in
\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
. Since
-\odot-
preserves colimits,
(A\odot A,q_{i}\odot q_{i})
is a colimiting cocone of
(-\odot-)D
thus its legs
q_{i}\odot q_{i}
are jointly epic. Therefore, from

\mu_{B}(\phi\odot\phi)(q_{i}\odot q_{i})=\mu_{B}(\phi q_{i}\odot\phi q_{i})=\mu_{B}(\phi_{i}\odot\phi_{i})=\phi_{i}\mu_{i}=\phi q_{i}\mu_{i}=\phi\mu(q_{i}\odot q_{i})

we get that
\mu_{B}(\phi\odot\phi)=\phi\mu
which means that
\phi
respects multiplication. With similar arguments, we get that
\phi
also respects the units, thus
\phi
is a monad map.

The fact that the forgetful functor creates these colimits is evident from the above construction.
∎

Remark 3.15
.

To see that the assumptions in
3.10
and
3.14
are indeed of a different nature, consider
\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathcal{C})
for a category with pullbacks
\mathcal{C}
. In this case the condition that
\mathbb{D}
have stable local
\mathcal{I}
-colimits translates to
\mathcal{C}
having
\mathcal{I}
-colimits which are stable under pullback. On the other hand,
-\odot-\colon\mathsf{Grph}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathsf{Grph}(\mathcal{C})
preserving
\mathcal{I}
-colimits would require the latter to
commute
with pullbacks in
\mathcal{C}
, a property which in general is distinct from pullback-stability.

We close this section by establishing local presentability for the category of monads
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
, for a locally presentable
\mathbb{D}
(
2.26
). Towards this end, we will need to impose extra conditions on
\mathbb{D}
concerning the preservation of filtered colimits by the unit and horizontal composition functors. The following proof essentially generalizes the approach for the category of monoids on a locally presentable and monoidal category, see
[
Por08
, 2.6]
.

Theorem 3.16
.

Let
\mathbb{D}
be a locally presentable double category where
-\odot-\colon\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})\to\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
and
1\colon\mathbb{D}_{0}\to\mathbb{D}_{1}
preserve filtered colimits. Then the category of monads
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
is locally presentable.

Proof.

(7)

Morphisms are 2-cells
{}^{f}\alpha^{f}\colon A\Rightarrow B
that commute with the above structure cells.
It is immediate that this endofunctor is finitary, since both
-\odot-
and
1_{(-)}
are by assumptions. Moreover, since
\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})
is locally presentable by
2.29
, it follows that
\mathsf{Alg}(F)
is also locally presentable, by standard facts about categories of algebras for an endofunctor, see e.g.
[
AMM25
, 11.3.2]
.

It is then the case that the category
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
of monads (
2.31
) can be viewed as a full subcategory of
\mathsf{Alg}(F)
: objects are
F
-algebras which on the one hand have globular structure 2-cells and on the other hand satisfy associativity and unitality axioms, and monad maps coincide with
F
-algebra maps. Hence, by showing that
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
is closed under limits and filtered colimits in
\mathsf{Alg}(F)
, the so-called “reflection theorem”
[
AR94
, 2.48]
will imply that
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
is itself a locally presentable category.

We begin with limits. Consider a diagram
D\colon\mathcal{I}\to\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
in
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})

and take its limit in
\mathsf{Alg}(F)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{i}}
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptstyle\Downarrow\lambda_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{i}}
\scriptstyle\bullet
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Di}

Since the locally presentable double category
\mathbb{D}
has all parallel limits, and
\mathsf{Alg}(F)\to\mathsf{End}(\mathbb{D})\to\mathbb{D}_{1}
creates all limits by standard endofunctor algebra facts and
2.21
,
the vertical components
(l_{i}\colon X\to X_{i})_{\in\mathcal{I}}
form a limit in
\mathbb{D}_{0}
and in particular the
l_{i}
’s form a jointly monomorphic family. To show that
(L,\mu,\eta)
is actually in
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{D})
, we must first show that
L
’s structure maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptstyle\Downarrow\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
